<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a deterministic algorithm that, for every finite family of explicitly represented ordinary strings, outputs a common superstring of length at most twice the optimum in time polynomial in the total encoded input size, including symbol labels. The guarantee applies to the algorithm constructed here, not the classical maximum-overlap Greedy procedure.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a deterministic algorithm, polynomial in the total encoded input size, that on every finite collection $\mathcal S$ of explicitly represented ordinary strings outputs a common superstring $T$ satisfying $$\lvert T\rvert\le 2\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S).$$
  - Explanation: The constructed algorithm returns a common superstring with a length bounded by twice the optimum; its running time is polynomial in the encoded input size.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-counts)): For every common superstring $T$ and every nonempty $s\in V$, $$m(s)\le N_T(s).$$ Consequently, for $$\label{eq:W} W=\sum_{c\in\Sigma}m(c),$$ we have $W\le\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S)$ and $m(s)\le L$.
  - Explanation: Descending forced counts lower-bound occurrences in every common superstring; their one-letter sum lower-bounds the optimum.
- **C3** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-base)): The graph $G$ is balanced, contains every required string as a used vertex, and has $W$ up edges and $W$ down edges, counted with multiplicity.
  - Explanation: Nonnegative edge multiplicities give a balanced base graph visiting every required string, with equal up and down totals.
- **C4** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-recorded)): The total number of recorded occurrences of $s$ on all the closed walks is $m(s)$.
  - Explanation: Recording occurrences on the closed walks recovers the forced counts exactly, even when words exceed a turn length.
- **C5** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-layers)): The walks of a group can be rearranged, without changing the multiset of their graph edges, into one-turn layers of period $p$. Each layer has an integer function $z:\mathbb Z\to\mathbb Z$ such that $$\label{eq:layer-properties} z(x-1)\le z(x),\qquad z(x-1)\ge x, \qquad z(x+p)=z(x)+p.$$ At start coordinate $x$, its windows are exactly $$\label{eq:layer-vertical} [x,e),\qquad z(x-1)\le e\le z(x);$$ it then takes a down step to start $x+1$. The functions of the layers in a group are pointwise ordered. Each layer has up cost $p$, and the sum of these costs over all groups is $W$.
  - Explanation: Sorting primitive passages produces pointwise ordered single-period layers while preserving all graph edges.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-matching-window)): Over all layers, the number of blocked occurrences of $s$ relative to $A$ is $B_A(s;m)$. Every unblocked occurrence is contained in an actual layer window that agrees with $A$ throughout.
  - Explanation: Blocking counts coincide with the blocking functional, and unblocked records admit actual fully matching windows.
- **C7** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-agreement)): Two distinct aligned periodic texts of periods $p,q$ agree on fewer than $p+q$ consecutive letters. If both have period $p$, they agree on fewer than $p$ consecutive letters.
  - Explanation: Distinct aligned periodic texts have strict agreement-length bounds; equality of aligned texts is excluded.
- **C8** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-max-rotation)): Let $A$ have least period $p$, and let $t$ be distinguished. Let $D$ be a distinct aligned periodic text of period $q$, all of whose forward rotations are lexicographically at most the forward word of $A$ at $t$. Then $D$ and $A$ cannot agree on all $q$ positions starting at $t$.
  - Explanation: A lexicographically maximal rotation obstructs a full target-period agreement with a distinct bounded-rotation text.
- **C9** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-ordered-windows)): Let $[a,b)$ and $[c,d)$ be windows in a common text over $\Sigma$, each spelling a word in $V$. If $a\le c$ and $b\le d$, there is a hierarchical-graph walk from the first word to the second of cost $d-b$. It visits a word of length at most $\max(0,b-c)$.* *Consequently an ordered list of windows, with both starts and ends nondecreasing, can be followed at cost equal to the total increase in its ends. If its last window is a translate of the first by $h$ in a text of period dividing $h$, this is a closed walk of cost $h$.
  - Explanation: Ordered actual windows in a shared text admit a hierarchical walk whose up cost is the increase in end coordinate.
- **C10** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-band)): Consider $k$ ordered layers from one primitive-text group of period $p$. At a start $t$, let $H$ be the top layer’s first end and $E$ the bottom layer’s last end. If $h$ is a nonnegative multiple of $p$ and $H-E\le h$, the layers can be joined together by a closed walk of cost at most $h$.
  - Explanation: An ordered band in one primitive-text group can be joined within a qualifying multiple of its period.
- **C11** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-collective)): Consider $k$ ordered layers with a common period-$p$ text $A$, and put $K=kp$. Let $R=[a,b)$ spell a word in $V$ agreeing with $A$. Let $r_H=f_H(a)$ be the top layer’s first end and $r_L=l_L(a)$ the bottom layer’s last end. If $$\label{eq:collective-conditions} r_H\le b+K,\qquad r_H-r_L\le K, \qquad r_L+K\ge b,$$ there is a cost-$K$ closed walk based at the word of $R$ that touches all $k$ layers.
  - Explanation: The three endpoint inequalities allow a closed collective link paid only from source-layer budgets.
- **C12** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-host)): Let a host layer have period $q$. On that layer choose actual windows $R_i=[a_i,b_i)$, indexed by a nonempty collection of distinct child groups. The text of child group $i$ has period $p_i$, is different up to translation from the host’s text and from every other child group’s text, and agrees with the host throughout $R_i$ in the chosen alignment.* *For each $i$, one or more child layers request a connection at this same record. Each has a specified actual window $[a_i,e)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:request-condition} a_i+p_i<e\le b_i-p_i.$$ Its period-$p_i$ budget is reserved for the request. If the host’s period-$q$ budget is also available, the host and all requesting layers can be rooted using at most these budgets.
  - Explanation: Distinct child groups satisfying the strict interior window condition can be rooted together using reserved child and host budgets.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-choose-k)): This procedure stops with $1\le k\le n$. If $L_*$ is the lowest of the top $k$ layers, then $$\begin{aligned} l_{L_*}(t)&\ge H-(k-1)p,\label{eq:sharp-band}\\ f_H(x)-l_{L_*}(x)&\le K\qquad(x\in\mathbb Z).\label{eq:wide-band} \end{aligned}$$ At most $k$ recorded occurrences of $w=A[t:H-K)$ have a full-text alignment identical to $A$.
  - Explanation: The threshold search stops within the upper-layer count, bounds band widths, and limits identical-alignment recorded occurrences.
- **C14** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-partition)): After all groups have been processed, every layer is either marked rooted or belongs to exactly one outgoing block. These two classes are disjoint. Each block consists of layers from one primitive-text group and targets a single actual layer. Its total budget remains reserved for its planned link. A collective block of $k$ layers and period $p$ also satisfies <a href="#eq:sharp-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-band">[eq:sharp-band]</a> at its distinguished position; an individual block consists of one layer.
  - Explanation: Group processing partitions layers into rooted layers and disjoint outgoing blocks with unspent link budgets.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-free-period)): A block of $k$ layers and period $p$ can be joined internally, in place of its outgoing link, for cost at most $(k-1)p$. Thus this replacement leaves at least $p$ of its total budget available.
  - Explanation: Replacing an outgoing link by internal joining releases at least one period of budget.
- **C16** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-cycle-contact)): On a directed cycle of blocks, choose a block whose group’s distinguished forward rotation is lexicographically greatest. If its target layer has period $q$, its planned outgoing link can be realized within its reserved budget so that the resulting connected piece contains a vertex of length at most $q$.
  - Explanation: A maximal distinguished rotation on a block cycle supplies a contact no longer than the target period.
- **C17** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-connection)): The base graph can be connected to $\varepsilon$ by adding closed walks of total up cost at most $W$.
  - Explanation: Closed additions connect the base graph to the empty word within the total additional layer budget.

```json
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-ordered-windows",
        "exact_quote": "Let $[a,b)$ and $[c,d)$ be windows in a common text over $\\Sigma$, each spelling a word in $V$. If $a\\le c$ and $b\\le d$, there is a hierarchical-graph walk from the first word to the second of cost $d-b$. It visits a word of length at most $\\max(0,b-c)$.*\n\n*Consequently an ordered list of windows, with both starts and ends nondecreasing, can be followed at cost equal to the total increase in its ends. If its last window is a translate of the first by $h$ in a text of period dividing $h$, this is a closed walk of cost $h$.",
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **common superstring** ([source](#sec:intro)): In the *shortest common superstring problem*, the input is a finite collection $\mathcal S$ of finite strings, and the task is to find a shortest string containing every member of $\mathcal S$ as a contiguous substring. Write $\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S)$ for its minimum possible length. The strings are represented explicitly, with encoded symbol labels and string boundaries. Let $N$ be the total bit length of this representation and $L$ the sum of the string lengths measured in symbols. Each symbol and boundary occupies at least one bit, so $L,\|\mathcal S\|\le N$; operations on symbol labels are charged for their encoded lengths.
- **OPT(S)** ([source](#sec:intro)): Write $\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S)$ for its minimum possible length.
- **N and L** ([source](#sec:intro)): Let $N$ be the total bit length of this representation and $L$ the sum of the string lengths measured in symbols.
- **Sigma and V** ([source](#sec:counts)): Let $\Sigma$ be its alphabet and let $V$ be the set of its distinct substrings, including the empty word $\varepsilon$.
- **periodic text** ([source](#sec:counts)): A *periodic text* is a map $A:\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$ with $A(i+p)=A(i)$ for some positive integer $p$.
- **blocking functional** ([source](#eq:block-functional)): Define the linear functional $$\label{eq:block-functional} B_A(s;m)=\sum_{r\in V\setminus\{\varepsilon\}}b_{A,s}(r)m(r).$$
- **forced occurrence counts** ([source](#eq:extension-rules)): Construct nonnegative integers $m(s)$ for $s\in V\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$ in decreasing order of length. Missing words have count zero.
- **hierarchical graph** ([source](#pm-h-9)): The *hierarchical graph* on $V$ has, for each nonempty $s$, an up edge $\operatorname{pref}(s)\to s$ and a down edge $s\to\operatorname{suf}(s)$, where $\operatorname{pref}$ deletes the last letter and $\operatorname{suf}$ deletes the first. The up edge has cost one and the down edge cost zero. Golovnev, Kulikov, and Mihajlin introduced a hierarchical graph for bounded-length inputs [[golovnev2014](#ref-golovnev2014)]; the all-substrings form and these edge costs appear in the Collapsing framework [[collapsing2020](#ref-collapsing2020), Section 2.2]. We give every graph property needed here directly.
- **base multiplicities** ([source](#eq:base-multiplicities)): Give the two edges at $s$ multiplicities $$\label{eq:base-multiplicities} u(s)=m(s)-\sum_c m(cs),\qquad d(s)=m(s)-\sum_c m(sc),$$ respectively.
- **recorded occurrence** ([source](#pm-h-11)): An occurrence interval of a nonempty word $s$ is *recorded* on the walk if some visited window contains it. Count occurrences modulo the walk’s turn length $P$, so each starting-position residue contributes at most once. Here the modulus is $P$, even if the text has a smaller least period.
- **distinguished position** ([source](#pm-h-14)): In a periodic group, a *distinguished position* $t$ starts its lexicographically greatest forward rotation.
- **rooted** ([source](#sec:connections)): A collection of layers and added walks is *rooted* if its graph support is connected to $\varepsilon$.
- **record** ([source](#pm-h-17)): We call a chosen actual layer window, together with an alignment to a specified periodic text agreeing throughout it, a *record*.
- **request** ([source](#pm-h-18)): A *request* reserves a source layer’s budget for an operation at a receiving layer, called its *host*.

```json
            "exact_quote": "Let $[a,b)$ and $[c,d)$ be windows in a common text over $\\Sigma$, each spelling a word in $V$. <a id="pm-s3"></a>
```
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-counts) / [proof](#sec:counts): Remove empty strings, duplicates and contained inputs; enumerate substring vertices and admissible periods. -> Reduced instance and substring universe
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-counts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-counts): Descending integer maxima of extension, required-word and period-rule bounds. -> Forced occurrence counts m and lower bound W
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-base) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-base): Assign nonnegative prefix-up and suffix-down multiplicities; telescope counts. -> Balanced base multigraph visiting required strings
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-recorded) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-recorded): Decompose into closed walks and lift visited words to text windows. -> Recorded occurrences equal m
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-layers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-layers): Group translation-equivalent texts and sort primitive passage exit ends. -> Ordered one-turn layers preserving every edge
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-matching-window) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matching-window): Use nearest mismatches and actual window transitions. -> Actual matching windows for unblocked records
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-prop-partition) / [proof](#sec:processing): Increasing-period processing: fulfill host requests, root easy bands, choose threshold, reserve collective/individual links. -> Rooted layers and disjoint budgeted outgoing blocks
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-thm-connection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-connection): Open functional graph cycles; retain maximal source link and free target budget by internal joining. -> All layers rooted with added up cost at most W
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-connection-2): Materialize closed additions and extract an Euler tour from the empty word using stable finite choices. -> Common superstring within stated factor bound; polynomial encoded-time procedure

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: The constructed algorithm returns a common superstring within twice the optimum.
- **I2**: Forced counts lower-bound occurrences in every common superstring.
- **I3**: The base graph is balanced with equal up/down multiplicity W.
- **I4**: Ordered windows can be followed at up cost d-b with a contact of length at most max(0,b-c).
- **I5**: Added closed walks connect the base graph to empty at total up cost at most W.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Transfer the factor-two result to classical maximum-overlap Greedy. -> The theorem does not certify that procedure.
- **A2**: Treat compressed or implicit sequence descriptions as admissible input. -> The bit-cost polynomial claim does not establish this regime.
- **A3**: Treat the executable ordered-window test as certification of the full approximation theorem. -> Counts, periodic layers, host requests and cycle surgery are not executed by Panel 3.
- **A4**: Use arbitrary intermediate windows outside the substring set. -> Graph edges may be absent.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. Finite constructive Lemma 9 subroutine only: materialize ordered-window walks. This does not implement or certify the full factor-two algorithm.

```python
# PM-P3-HEADER: headers added deterministically from panel3 fields (missing in the professor code)
# INPUTS: JSON list of valid ordered windows in finite explicit texts. V is all substrings of each text, including empty. Input errors exit nonzero.
# PRECONDITIONS: JSON list of valid ordered windows in finite explicit texts. V is all substrings of each text, including empty. Input errors exit nonzero.
# OUTPUTS: JSON list of vertex paths and exact up costs d-b; each path ends at the second word and visits a word of length <= max(0,b-c).
# TIME: ABSENT (not stated in panel3)
# SPACE: ABSENT (not stated in panel3)
# ARITHMETIC: ABSENT (not stated in panel3)
# FAILURE: Gap case has up cost less than d-b.; Witness contains a non-down graph transition or wrong endpoint.; Reported cost differs from exact graph edge sum.
"""Lemma 9: materialize ordered-window walks, not the whole approximation algorithm.
Input: JSON list of {text,a,b,c,d}, ordered valid half-open windows.
V is all substrings of text, including empty; alphabet is that of text.
Output: JSON witnesses with vertices and exact integer up cost.
Time/space: linear in materialized walk and input per case.
Arithmetic: flint fmpz for cost; invalid input raises (nonzero exit).
"""
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz

def solve(v):
    text = v['text']
    a, b, c, d = (v[k] for k in ('a','b','c','d'))
    assert 0 <= a <= b <= len(text) and 0 <= c <= d <= len(text)
    assert a <= c and b <= d
    current = text[a:b]
    path = [current]
    cost = fmpz(0)
    def down():
        nonlocal current
        current = current[1:]
        path.append(current)
    def up(letter):
        nonlocal current, cost
        current += letter
        path.append(current)
        cost += 1
    if c <= b:
        for _ in range(c-a):
            down()
        for letter in text[b:d]:
            up(letter)
    else:
        for _ in range(b-a):
            down()
        for letter in text[b:c]:
            up(letter)
            down()
        for letter in text[c:d]:
            up(letter)
    return {'path':path, 'cost':int(cost)}

print(json.dumps([solve(v) for v in json.load(sys.stdin)]))
```

```json
    "oracle_code": "\"\"\"Independent graph-language oracle; never imports the implementation.\nEnumerates the finite hierarchical graph, checks each witness exactly, and\nindependently decides existence of a bounded-cost walk via state exploration.\n\"\"\"\nimport json, sys\nfrom collections import deque\nfrom flint import fmpz\n\ninputs = json.load(open(sys.argv[1]))\noutputs = json.load(open(sys.argv[2]))\nassert sys.argv[3] == 'ORDERED_WINDOWS_VALID'\nassert len(inputs) == len(outputs)\nfor v, out in zip(inputs, outputs):\n    text = v['text']\n    a,b,c,d = (v[k] for k in ('a','b','c','d'))\n    assert 0 <= a <= b <= len(text) and 0 <= c <= d <= len(text)\n    assert a <= c and b <= d\n    words = {text[i:j] for i in range(len(text)+1) for j in range(i,len(text)+1)}\n    edges = {w:[] for w in words}\n    for w in words - {''}:\n        edges[w[:-1]].append((w,1))\n        edges[w].append((w[1:],0))\n    path = out['path']\n    assert path and path[0] == text[a:b] and path[-1] == text[c:d]\n    assert all(w in words for w in path)\n    cost = fmpz(0)\n    for x,y in zip(path,path[1:]):\n        choices = [k for z,k in edges[x] if z == y]\n        assert len(choices) == 1\n        cost += choices[0]\n    bound = fmpz(d)-fmpz(b)\n    assert cost == bound == fmpz(out['cost'])\n    short = max(0,b-c)\n    assert min(map(len,path)) <= short\n    # Exhaustive state search in the graph, with exact cost and contact flag.\n    start = (text[a:b],0,len(text[a:b]) <= short)\n    seen = {start}\n    queue = deque([start])\n    while queue:\n        word, used, touched = queue.popleft()\n        for target, delta in edges[word]:\n            nxt = (target,used+delta,touched or len(target) <= short)\n            if nxt[1] <= int(bound) and nxt not in seen:\n                seen.add(nxt)\n                queue.append(nxt)\n    assert (text[c:d],int(bound),True) in seen\n",
    "output_contract": "JSON list of vertex paths and exact up costs d-b; each path ends at the second word and visits a word of length <= max(0,b-c).",
    "reason": "Finite constructive Lemma 9 subroutine only: materialize ordered-window walks. This does not implement or certify the full factor-two algorithm.",
    "reference_code": "# PM-P3-HEADER: headers added deterministically from panel3 fields (missing in the professor code)\n# INPUTS: JSON list of valid ordered windows in finite explicit texts. V is all substrings of each text, including empty. Input errors exit nonzero.\n# PRECONDITIONS: JSON list of valid ordered windows in finite explicit texts. V is all substrings of each text, including empty. Input errors exit nonzero.\n# OUTPUTS: JSON list of vertex paths and exact up costs d-b; each path ends at the second word and visits a word of length <= max(0,b-c).\n# TIME: ABSENT (not stated in panel3)\n# SPACE: ABSENT (not stated in panel3)\n# ARITHMETIC: ABSENT (not stated in panel3)\n# FAILURE: Gap case has up cost less than d-b.; Witness contains a non-down graph transition or wrong endpoint.; Reported cost differs from exact graph edge sum.\n\"\"\"Lemma 9: materialize ordered-window walks, not the whole approximation algorithm.\nInput: JSON list of {text,a,b,c,d}, ordered valid half-open windows.\nV is all substrings of text, including empty; alphabet is that of text.\nOutput: JSON witnesses with vertices and exact integer up cost.\nTime/space: linear in materialized walk and input per case.\nArithmetic: flint fmpz for cost; invalid input raises (nonzero exit).\n\"\"\"\nimport json, sys\nfrom flint import fmpz\n\ndef solve(v):\n    text = v['text']\n    a, b, c, d = (v[k] for k in ('a','b','c','d'))\n    assert 0 <= a <= b <= len(text) and 0 <= c <= d <= len(text)\n    assert a <= c and b <= d\n    current = text[a:b]\n    path = [current]\n    cost = fmpz(0)\n    def down():\n        nonlocal current\n        current = current[1:]\n        path.append(current)\n    def up(letter):\n        nonlocal current, cost\n        current += letter\n        path.append(current)\n        cost += 1\n    if c <= b:\n        for _ in range(c-a):\n            down()\n        for letter in text[b:d]:\n            up(letter)\n    else:\n        for _ in range(b-a):\n            down()\n        for letter in text[b:c]:\n            up(letter)\n            down()\n        for letter in text[c:d]:\n            up(letter)\n    return {'path':path, 'cost':int(cost)}\n\nprint(json.dumps([solve(v) for v in json.load(sys.stdin)]))\n",
    "test_vectors": [
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | strong | implementable | inferred | A software implementation may synthesize a common superstring under the exact explicit-string contract; applicability does not establish production readiness. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Game mechanics, simulation dynamics and fidelity criteria are ABSENT; the source problem is finite contiguous-string containment. |
| tools | True | strong | implementable | inferred | An offline string-combination tool can expose the paper construction when its requested artifact is a common superstring; the tool packaging is an inferred engineering use. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The deterministic polynomial-time common-superstring approximation is the stated algorithmic result; the domain mapping is direct. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | The paper explicitly specifies substring dictionaries, edge-copy adjacency lists and finite periodic-layer records needed to materialize the construction; no general-purpose data-structure performance guarantee follows. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Accelerator architecture, parallel scheduling, throughput and device resource bounds are ABSENT; encoded-label costs are accounted for by a deterministic finite construction. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Security definitions, adversaries and cryptographic reductions are ABSENT; minimizing a containing string supplies no security property. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Statistical estimation, training, generalization and learning objectives are ABSENT; the construction is deterministic on explicit strings. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The source objective is minimum symbol length subject to containing every input contiguously; its constructed algorithm supplies an approximation contract for this specific discrete optimization problem. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Communication, failures, replication and consensus guarantees are ABSENT; the explicit graph construction does not supply a distributed protocol. |
| other (combinatorics_on_words) | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | The paper gives finite periodic-word agreement and rotation arguments that can support exact comparisons inside this superstring construction; broader applications need their own preconditions. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **LIMIT-01** (hypothesis): The theorem covers finite collections of explicitly represented ordinary strings. Extension to compressed, implicit, infinite, wildcard or approximate-match inputs requires a separate proof. Blocked use: ['Apply the guarantee to generalized inputs without a proved reduction.']
- **LIMIT-02** (approximation): The output is a common superstring with the stated approximation bound; exact optimality is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Treat the returned string as a certified shortest superstring.']
- **LIMIT-03** (counterexample_scope): The guarantee does not transfer to classical maximum-overlap Greedy or prescribed Collapsing normalization. Blocked use: ['Certify a Greedy implementation using this theorem.', 'Certify a Collapsing implementation using this theorem.']
- **LIMIT-04** (hypothesis): Polynomial time is measured in the total encoded input size, including symbol labels, rather than an unbounded-label unit-cost model. Blocked use: ['Ignore variable-width symbol comparison and output costs.']
- **LIMIT-05** (edge_case): Preprocessing removes empty strings, duplicates and contained inputs; if no input remains, the output is empty. Subsequent nonempty-instance constructions must follow this branch. Blocked use: ['Run the nonempty layer procedure on an empty reduced instance.']
- **LIMIT-06** (hypothesis): The substring vertex universe must be closed under taking substrings; replacing it by only input strings omits needed intermediate vertices. Blocked use: ['Use an input-only vertex set for the hierarchical walks.']
- **LIMIT-07** (hypothesis): Enumerating only least-period templates is not the described count recursion; the paper includes nonleast periods. Blocked use: ['Drop nonleast-period count rules without a separate equivalence proof.']
- **LIMIT-08** (hypothesis): Extension inequalities alone do not implement the forced counts: the periodicity rules capture repetitions that they miss. Blocked use: ['Omit periodicity rules and still claim the lower-bound/base-graph construction.']
- **LIMIT-09** (hypothesis): Equal-period group membership is up to text translation; records compare a specific alignment, which can differ even within a group. Blocked use: ['Identify all phases of a primitive group as the same aligned text.']
- **LIMIT-10** (hypothesis): Budget assignments must follow the described connection and cycle-replacement operations; coincidental connectivity does not release budgets automatically. Blocked use: ['Spend one layer budget on multiple independent connection requests.']
- **LIMIT-11** (hypothesis): The final Euler extraction requires balanced connected used support containing the empty word; a balanced disconnected base graph alone is insufficient. Blocked use: ['Extract a guaranteed common superstring from a single component of the unconnected base graph.']
- **LIMIT-12** (unknown): Practical wall-clock performance, tight implementation complexity exponents and accelerated/distributed execution guarantees are ABSENT from the supplied theorem. Blocked use: ['Advertise measured throughput, a specific polynomial exponent, GPU speedup or distributed correctness based solely on this paper.']
- **LIMIT-13** (formal_scope): A finite test of the descending counts or base graph does not certify the full connection construction or the approximation theorem. Blocked use: ['Present a subroutine oracle receipt as a proof of the complete approximation algorithm.']

>
> ## Scope
>
> The shortest common superstring problem asks for the shortest string containing each input string as a contiguous substring. The formalized result gives one deterministic polynomial-time algorithm whose output is a common superstring of length at most twice the unrestricted optimum. It applies to every finite list of explicitly encoded strings, with running time measured in input bits and the approximation ratio measured in symbol length.

Comparator source `Superstring.lean` (1549 bytes, sha256 `8930f17794dfd3ca55e5c238019e5ccecf8c189d2df87df83773d00d1f4d1d3a`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace Superstring

abbrev Symbol := List Bool
abbrev Word := List Symbol
abbrev Instance := List Word

/-- Prefix markers encode the list spine, including its final boundary. -/
def encodeList {α : Type} (enc : α → List Bool) : List α → List Bool
  | [] => [true]
  | a :: as => false :: (enc a ++ encodeList enc as)

def encodeSymbol : Symbol → List Bool := encodeList (fun b => [b])
def encodeWord : Word → List Bool := encodeList encodeSymbol
def encodeInstance : Instance → List Bool := encodeList encodeWord

/-- Ordinary contiguous substring containment, including the empty word. -/
def IsCommonSuperstring (S : Instance) (T : Word) : Prop :=
  ∀ s ∈ S, s <:+: T

/-- The optimum in symbols, not in bits. -/
noncomputable def opt (S : Instance) : ℕ :=
  sInf {n : ℕ | ∃ T : Word, IsCommonSuperstring S T ∧ T.length = n}

/-- A deterministic finite-stack-machine implementation, with a polynomial bound
on bit-level running time in the explicitly encoded input length. -/
def HasPolynomialImplementation (f : Instance → Word) : Prop :=
  ∃ M : Turing.TM2ComputableInPolyTime encodeInstance encodeWord f,
    ∀ k : M.tm.K, Finite (M.tm.Γ k)

/-- The deterministic algorithm with its exact finite-alphabet bit-machine
certificate, contiguous coverage, and factor-two bound in symbols. -/
theorem main : ∃ f : Instance → Word,
    HasPolynomialImplementation f ∧
    ∀ S, IsCommonSuperstring S (f S) ∧ (f S).length ≤ 2 * opt S := by
  sorry

end Superstring

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a deterministic algorithm that, for every finite family of explicitly represented ordinary strings, outputs a common superstring of length at most twice the optimum in time polynomial in the total encoded input size, including symbol labels. The guarantee applies to the algorithm constructed here, not the classical maximum-overlap Greedy procedure.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 1 | There is a deterministic algorithm, polynomial in the total encoded input size, that on every finite collection $\mathcal S$ of explicitly represented ordinary strings outputs a common superstring $T$ |
| [stmt-lem-counts](#stmt-lem-counts) | lemma | `lem:counts` | 2 | For every common superstring $T$ and every nonempty $s\in V$, $$m(s)\le N_T(s).$$ Consequently, for |
| [stmt-lem-base](#stmt-lem-base) | lemma | `lem:base` | 3 | The graph $G$ is balanced, contains every required string as a used vertex, and has $W$ up edges and $W$ down edges, counted with multiplicity.* |
| [stmt-lem-recorded](#stmt-lem-recorded) | lemma | `lem:recorded` | 4 | The total number of recorded occurrences of $s$ on all the closed walks is $m(s)$.* |
| [stmt-lem-layers](#stmt-lem-layers) | lemma | `lem:layers` | 5 | The walks of a group can be rearranged, without changing the multiset of their graph edges, into one-turn layers of period $p$. |
| [stmt-lem-matching-window](#stmt-lem-matching-window) | lemma | `lem:matching-window` | 6 | Over all layers, the number of blocked occurrences of $s$ relative to $A$ is $B_A(s;m)$. |
| [stmt-lem-agreement](#stmt-lem-agreement) | lemma | `lem:agreement` | 7 | Two distinct aligned periodic texts of periods $p,q$ agree on fewer than $p+q$ consecutive letters. |
| [stmt-lem-max-rotation](#stmt-lem-max-rotation) | lemma | `lem:max-rotation` | 8 | Let $A$ have least period $p$, and let $t$ be distinguished. |
| [stmt-lem-ordered-windows](#stmt-lem-ordered-windows) | lemma | `lem:ordered-windows` | 9 | Let $[a,b)$ and $[c,d)$ be windows in a common text over $\Sigma$, each spelling a word in $V$. |
| [stmt-lem-band](#stmt-lem-band) | lemma | `lem:band` | 10 | Consider $k$ ordered layers from one primitive-text group of period $p$. |
| [stmt-lem-collective](#stmt-lem-collective) | lemma | `lem:collective` | 11 | Consider $k$ ordered layers with a common period-$p$ text $A$, and put $K=kp$. |

<a id="sec:intro"></a>
### Introduction

In the *shortest common superstring problem*, the input is a finite collection $\mathcal S$ of finite strings, and the task is to find a shortest string containing every member of $\mathcal S$ as a contiguous substring. Write $\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S)$ for its minimum possible length. The strings are represented explicitly, with encoded symbol labels and string boundaries. Let $N$ be the total bit length of this representation and $L$ the sum of the string lengths measured in symbols. Each symbol and boundary occupies at least one bit, so $L,|\mathcal S|\le N$; operations on symbol labels are charged for their encoded lengths.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a deterministic algorithm, polynomial in the total encoded input size, that on every finite collection $\mathcal S$ of explicitly represented ordinary strings outputs a common superstring $T$ satisfying $$\lvert T\rvert\le 2\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S).$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### History and significance.

Tarhio and Ukkonen proposed the factor-$2$ conjecture for the maximum-overlap Greedy procedure <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[tarhio1988](#ref-tarhio1988)]. Blum, Jiang, Li, Tromp, and Yannakakis established a polynomial-time $3$-approximation <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[blum1994](#ref-blum1994)]; Sweedyk later obtained a $5/2$ guarantee <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[sweedyk1999](#ref-sweedyk1999)]. Subsequent guarantees include $2+11/23$ due to Mucha <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[mucha2013](#ref-mucha2013)] and $(14+\sqrt{67})/9<2.466$ due to Englert, Matsakis, and Veselý <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[emv2023](#ref-emv2023)]. An August 2026 preprint of Chukhin, Kulikov, Mihajlin, and Smal reports a $7/3$-approximation <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[cycle2026](#ref-cycle2026)]. [thm:main](#thm:main) answers affirmatively the question of whether a polynomial-time factor-$2$ approximation exists.

Many earlier approximation algorithms use the distance graph on the input strings, whose edge costs are the lengths of prefixes left after maximum suffix–prefix overlap. A minimum-weight cycle cover gives a computable lower bound, but its cycles must then be opened and joined while controlling the extra length <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[blum1994](#ref-blum1994)]. Breslauer, Jiang, and Jiang use rotations of periodic strings to control overlaps <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[breslauer1997](#ref-breslauer1997)]; Mucha sharpens this analysis through lexicographically extremal primitive rotations <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[mucha2013](#ref-mucha2013)]. These provide conceptual precedents for the periodic comparisons below; our lower bound and the graph to which we apply them are different.

Our graph representation comes from another formulation. Golovnev, Kulikov, and Mihajlin introduced a hierarchical graph in the bounded-length setting <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[golovnev2014](#ref-golovnev2014)], and the Collapsing framework developed its all-substrings form <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[collapsing2020](#ref-collapsing2020)]. In this graph, appending or deleting a letter gives an edge between substring vertices, and a superstring is represented by a closed walk visiting the required strings. The separation between a balanced graph and the operations that connect it also appears in the hierarchical cycle-cover discussion of that framework <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[collapsing2020](#ref-collapsing2020), revised version, Section 7.2]. We use this representation to turn forced occurrence counts into a balanced graph and then connect it within the same cost bound.

The factor-$2$ question has also motivated conjectures about specific procedures. The classical Greedy conjecture concerns repeatedly merging a pair of maximum overlap, while the Collapsing conjecture concerns a prescribed normalization of doubled hierarchical-graph walks <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[collapsing2020](#ref-collapsing2020)]. A September 2026 preprint of Shibata reports a counterexample to the Greedy conjecture, with worst-case ratio at least $9/4$ <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[greedy2026](#ref-greedy2026)]. Our theorem concerns the algorithm proved here; neither of those prescribed procedures is assumed to attain factor $2$.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Proof overview.

The vertices of our hierarchical graph are all substrings of the inputs, including the empty word. An up edge appends a letter and costs one; a down edge deletes the first letter and costs zero. A balanced multiset of edges has equal incoming and outgoing degree at each vertex. If its support is connected and visits the empty word and every required string, an Euler tour spells a common superstring whose length is its up-edge cost.

We first construct integer counts $m(s)$ no larger than the number of occurrences of $s$ in any common superstring. Their one-letter sum $W$ is therefore a lower bound on the optimum. Counts for longer words constrain those of their prefixes and suffixes. A further rule handles periodic words. Relative to a fixed periodic text, a bracketing word consists of a matching interval with a mismatching letter at each end. When the assigned count of a long word exceeds the contribution of its bracketing words, a prefix shorter by $k$ periods must have count at least $k+1$ above its own bracketing contribution. The resulting counts determine a balanced, possibly disconnected graph of cost $W$.

Next decompose that graph into closed walks. The letters appended during a walk, repeated in both directions, form a periodic text; each visited vertex is a finite interval of that text. Walks with the same periodic text can be rearranged, preserving every edge, into ordered walks that each traverse one least period. We call these walks *layers*. A layer of period $p$ has cost $p$ and receives a further budget $p$, so all additional budgets together equal $W$. In the remaining case of the connection procedure, the count rule forces a shorter word to occur on a layer with a different aligned text. A visited interval agreeing with both texts gives an actual graph vertex at which to attach a connection.

The final task is to arrange these connections without spending a budget twice. Some closed walks connect their source layers to a target using only the source budgets. Others require a contribution from the target, but these requests go only to a strictly larger period and are handled together when that period is processed. For each connection paid for only by its source layers, those layers can instead be joined internally while saving one period of budget. The remaining source-funded connections may form directed cycles. On each cycle, a maximal lexicographic rotation yields a contact word no longer than the target’s period. Omitting that target’s outgoing connection and joining its layers internally therefore pays for a round trip from the contact to the empty word. This connects the base graph at additional cost at most $W$ and completes the factor-$2$ bound.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Organization.

[sec:counts](#sec:counts) constructs the counts and their balanced graph. [sec:layers](#sec:layers) develops the layer representation and the periodicity facts. [sec:connections](#sec:connections) gives the connection operations, [sec:processing](#sec:processing) processes groups of equal primitive text, and [sec:cycles](#sec:cycles) resolves the remaining cycles. [sec:algorithm](#sec:algorithm) proves polynomial time and extracts the output string.

<a id="sec:counts"></a>
### Forced occurrence counts

Delete the empty string, duplicates, and every input string contained in another input string. This preserves the set of common superstrings and its optimum. If nothing remains, output the empty string. Henceforth the remaining instance is nonempty. Let $\Sigma$ be its alphabet and let $V$ be the set of its distinct substrings, including the empty word $\varepsilon$. Thus $V$ is closed under taking substrings, $\lvert V\rvert=O(L^2)$, and every required string is maximal under extension in $V$.

For a finite string $s$, indices start at zero. Write $s[i:j)$ for its substring on the indicated half-open interval. A *periodic text* is a map $A:\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$ with $A(i+p)=A(i)$ for some positive integer $p$. The period need not be least unless this is specified.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Blocking an occurrence

<a id="eq:block-interior"></a>
<a id="eq:block-ends"></a>
<a id="eq:block-functional"></a>
Fix a text $A$ and a nonempty $s$ such that $A[0:\lvert s\rvert)=s$. For $r\in V$, let $b_{A,s}(r)$ be the number of integers $j$ with $$1\le j,\qquad j+\lvert s\rvert\le\lvert r\rvert-1,$$ for which $r[j:j+\lvert s\rvert)=s$ and $$\begin{aligned}
r(h)&=A(h-j) &&(1\le h\le\lvert r\rvert-2),\label{eq:block-interior}\\
r(0)&\ne A(-j),\qquad
r(\lvert r\rvert-1)\ne A(\lvert r\rvert-1-j).\label{eq:block-ends}
\end{aligned}$$ The word $r$ consists of a matching interval bracketed by one mismatching letter on each side of the specified $s$. Define the linear functional $$\label{eq:block-functional}
B_A(s;m)=\sum_{r\in V\setminus\{\varepsilon\}}b_{A,s}(r)m(r).$$ Only words with $\lvert r\rvert\ge\lvert s\rvert+2$ contribute.

<a id="eq:block-count"></a>
If an occurrence of $s$ in a finite word $T$ is aligned with $A$, its nearest mismatch on each side, when both exist, determines a unique bracketing word $r$. Call the occurrence *blocked* if this word belongs to $V$, and *unblocked* otherwise. In particular, an occurrence with no mismatch on one side is unblocked. When $N_T(r)$ denotes the number of occurrences of $r$ in $T$, uniqueness of the nearest mismatches gives $$\label{eq:block-count}
B_A(s;N_T)=\#\{\text{blocked occurrences of }s\text{ in }T\}
\le N_T(s).$$ The multiplicity in $b_{A,s}(r)$ is necessary: a single occurrence of $r$ may bracket several distinct occurrences of $s$.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The rules

<a id="eq:extension-rules"></a>
<a id="eq:period-rule"></a>
Construct nonnegative integers $m(s)$ for $s\in V\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$ in decreasing order of length. Missing words have count zero. At each $s$, take the smallest integer satisfying $$\label{eq:extension-rules}
m(s)\ge\sum_{c\in\Sigma}m(cs),\qquad
m(s)\ge\sum_{c\in\Sigma}m(sc),$$ and $m(s)\ge1$ if $s$ is required. Impose also the following rules for every period-$p$ text $A$, every $v=A[0:\lvert v\rvert)\in V$, and every nonempty prefix $w$ of $v$ with $\lvert v\rvert=\lvert w\rvert+kp$, $k\ge1$: $$\label{eq:period-rule}
m(v)>B_A(v;m)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
m(w)\ge B_A(w;m)+k+1.$$ All quantities constraining $m(w)$ have already been determined: $v$ is longer than $w$, and every term of either blocking functional is longer than its inner word. Thus these are explicit lower bounds in a descending recursion.

The apparent range over periodic texts is finite for this purpose. In a rule, $p<\lvert v\rvert$, and $A$ is determined by the first $p$ letters of $v$. One may enumerate $v$, $p$, and $k$, check that repetition of the first $p$ letters agrees with $v$, and retain the admissible rules. This includes nonleast periods.

<a id="stmt-lem-counts"></a>
<div id="lem:counts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Occurrence lower bound). *For every common superstring $T$ and every nonempty $s\in V$, $$m(s)\le N_T(s).$$ Consequently, for <a id="eq:W"></a>$$\label{eq:W}
W=\sum_{c\in\Sigma}m(c),$$ we have $W\le\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S)$ and $m(s)\le L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-counts</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $T$ and abbreviate $N_T$ to $N$. Induct on decreasing word length. The counts $N$ satisfy <a href="#eq:extension-rules" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extension-rules">[eq:extension-rules]</a>: occurrences with different preceding letters are disjoint, as are those with different following letters. They also satisfy the requirement for input strings.

Consider a rule <a href="#eq:period-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:period-rule">[eq:period-rule]</a> constraining $m(w)$ whose trigger holds. If $N(v)>B_A(v;N)$, choose an unblocked occurrence of $v$. It contains occurrences of $w$ at offsets $0,p,\ldots,kp$. Each is unblocked. Indeed, because $v$ matches $A$ and the offsets are multiples of $p$, a nearest mismatch bracketing one of these shorter occurrences must lie outside $v$. A blocking word in $V$ for it would therefore also block the chosen occurrence of $v$. The $k+1$ shorter occurrences are distinct, so <a href="#eq:block-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-count">[eq:block-count]</a> and induction give $$N(w)\ge B_A(w;N)+k+1\ge B_A(w;m)+k+1.$$

In the other case, <a href="#eq:block-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-count">[eq:block-count]</a> implies $$B_A(v;N)=N(v)\ge m(v)>B_A(v;m).$$ Every difference $N(r)-m(r)$ in this blocking sum is nonnegative by induction. Some contributing word $r$ therefore has $N(r)-m(r)\ge1$. Choose one of its counted placements of $v$. The $k+1$ period-spaced prefixes $w$ within that placement give distinct counted placements of $w$ in the same $r$. Hence $b_{A,w}(r)\ge k+1$. All other terms in the difference of blocking sums are nonnegative, and therefore $$N(w)\ge B_A(w;N)\ge B_A(w;m)+k+1.$$ Every lower bound used to define $m(w)$ is thus at most $N(w)$, completing the induction.

For an optimal $T$, summing over one-letter words gives $W\le\lvert T\rvert$. For a concatenation of the inputs, $N_T(s)\le\lvert T\rvert\le L$, giving the second assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The balanced base graph

The *hierarchical graph* on $V$ has, for each nonempty $s$, an up edge $\operatorname{pref}(s)\to s$ and a down edge $s\to\operatorname{suf}(s)$, where $\operatorname{pref}$ deletes the last letter and $\operatorname{suf}$ deletes the first. The up edge has cost one and the down edge cost zero. Golovnev, Kulikov, and Mihajlin introduced a hierarchical graph for bounded-length inputs <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[golovnev2014](#ref-golovnev2014)]; the all-substrings form and these edge costs appear in the Collapsing framework <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[collapsing2020](#ref-collapsing2020), Section 2.2]. We give every graph property needed here directly.

<a id="eq:base-multiplicities"></a>
Give the two edges at $s$ multiplicities $$\label{eq:base-multiplicities}
u(s)=m(s)-\sum_c m(cs),\qquad
d(s)=m(s)-\sum_c m(sc),$$ respectively. Write $G$ for the resulting directed multigraph, omitting unused edges and vertices.

<a id="stmt-lem-base"></a>
<div id="lem:base" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *The graph $G$ is balanced, contains every required string as a used vertex, and has $W$ up edges and $W$ down edges, counted with multiplicity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-base"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-base</summary>

*Proof.* The multiplicities are nonnegative by <a href="#eq:extension-rules" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extension-rules">[eq:extension-rules]</a>. At a nonempty $s$, the incoming and outgoing degrees are, respectively, $$u(s)+\sum_c d(cs),\qquad d(s)+\sum_c u(sc),$$ and both simplify to $m(s)-\sum_{c,d}m(csd)$. Balance at $\varepsilon$ follows by summing degrees over the other vertices. Telescoping <a href="#eq:base-multiplicities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:base-multiplicities">[eq:base-multiplicities]</a> over all nonempty words gives $\sum_s u(s)=\sum_s d(s)=\sum_c m(c)=W$. Finally, a remaining required string has no proper extension in $V$, so both its edge multiplicities equal its positive count. ◻

</details>

For example, suppose the only required word is $a^h$, with $h\ge2$. The extension inequalities alone allow $m(a^j)=1$ for $1\le j\le h$. Their base graph is only the two-edge walk between $a^{h-1}$ and $a^h$, with cost $1$. In the constant text $a$, every blocking functional is zero. Applying the period-one rule to $v=a^h$ and $w=a^j$ forces $m(a^j)\ge h-j+1$ for $1\le j<h$; the terminal requirement gives the same bound for $j=h$. The occurrence lower bound gives equality. The resulting graph contains both directions of the entire path from $\varepsilon$ to $a^h$, and $W=h$. Thus the periodicity rules record the repeated letters that the extension inequalities alone miss.

We retain all base edges throughout. Adding closed walks preserves balance. To prove [thm:main](#thm:main), it remains to connect every component of $G$ to $\varepsilon$ at additional up cost at most $W$.

<a id="sec:layers"></a>
### Periodic layers

Our goal is to replace the closed walks of the base graph by ordered layers without changing any edge. Their actual windows will later provide the occurrence records and same-text joins used to spend the second $W$ budget.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Lifting closed walks to windows

Decompose the balanced base graph into nonempty closed walks. Every such walk has the same positive number of up and down steps: the word length changes by $+1$ or $-1$ at each step and returns to its starting value. Read the letters appended on the up steps cyclically, and repeat this circular word in both directions to obtain a periodic text.

On the infinite repetition of the walk, represent a vertex by a window $[x,e)$ of this text. An up step increases $e$ by one; a down step increases $x$ by one. The window spells the vertex word. To verify this last assertion, follow the repeated walk sufficiently far back: its down steps eventually remove every letter previously present, so each current letter must have entered at one of the indicated up steps. A closed walk with $P$ up steps advances both coordinates by $P$.

An occurrence interval of a nonempty word $s$ is *recorded* on the walk if some visited window contains it. Count occurrences modulo the walk’s turn length $P$, so each starting-position residue contributes at most once. Here the modulus is $P$, even if the text has a smaller least period.

<a id="stmt-lem-recorded"></a>
<div id="lem:recorded" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *The total number of recorded occurrences of $s$ on all the closed walks is $m(s)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-recorded"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-recorded</summary>

*Proof.* At the unique up step reaching the right endpoint of a given occurrence, the current window contains the occurrence if and only if it is recorded. Indeed, subsequent left endpoints only increase, so a later containing window implies containment at that step as well. The up step generates a word ending in $s$. Summing its multiplicity over all such words gives $$\sum_{r\in V:\ r\text{ ends in }s}u(r)=m(s)$$ by telescoping over left extensions in <a href="#eq:base-multiplicities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:base-multiplicities">[eq:base-multiplicities]</a>. The argument is on the infinite lift and therefore also applies when $\lvert s\rvert$ exceeds a turn length. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Sorting primitive passages

Group the walks by their periodic texts up to integer translation, and align the texts within each group. Group membership is up to translation, while a later record compares a specified coordinate alignment of two layers; that alignment can differ even within one group. Write $p$ for the least positive period of a group. Every original turn length is a multiple of $p$: the shifts preserving a two-sided text form a subgroup of $\mathbb Z$, generated by its least positive member.

<a id="stmt-lem-layers"></a>
<div id="lem:layers" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:layer-properties"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Ordered layers). *The walks of a group can be rearranged, without changing the multiset of their graph edges, into one-turn layers of period $p$. Each layer has an integer function $z:\mathbb Z\to\mathbb Z$ such that $$\label{eq:layer-properties}
z(x-1)\le z(x),\qquad z(x-1)\ge x,
\qquad z(x+p)=z(x)+p.$$ At start coordinate $x$, its windows are exactly <a id="eq:layer-vertical"></a>$$\label{eq:layer-vertical}
[x,e),\qquad z(x-1)\le e\le z(x);$$ it then takes a down step to start $x+1$. The functions of the layers in a group are pointwise ordered. Each layer has up cost $p$, and the sum of these costs over all groups is $W$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-layers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-layers</summary>

*Proof.* For original walk $j$, let $P_j=d_jp$ be its turn length and let $Z_j(x)$ be its exit end at start $x$ on the infinite lift. Thus $Z_j(x+P_j)=Z_j(x)+P_j$. At each integer start $x$, collect the normalized exit ends $$\mathcal E_x=
\bigl\{Z_j(x+rp)-rp:\ j,\ 0\le r<d_j\bigr\},$$ with multiplicity. These are the passages through the same start residue, translated to start $x$. Translation by $rp$ preserves their word labels. Sort $\mathcal E_x$ increasingly and call its order statistics $z_1(x),\ldots,z_h(x)$, where $h=\sum_j d_j$.

The corresponding entering ends at $x$ form $\mathcal E_{x-1}$. Each original exit is at least its paired entry, so the increasing order statistics also satisfy $z_i(x-1)\le z_i(x)$. Every entry is at least $x$. Moreover, increasing $x$ by $p$ permutes the passages from each original walk and adds $p$ to their ends. Thus $\mathcal E_{x+p}=\mathcal E_x+p$, proving all of <a href="#eq:layer-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:layer-properties">[eq:layer-properties]</a>.

The multiset of down steps is fixed by the exit-end multisets. At a fixed $x$, the number of copies of a vertical unit edge with lower end $e$ is the number of entries at or below $e$ minus the number of exits at or below $e$. Thus the up-edge multiset is unchanged as well. The sorted passages therefore use exactly the original graph edges, with multiplicity, and in particular only actual base vertices. Each layer advances both coordinates by $p$ in one turn and has $p$ up steps. Their total up cost remains $W$. ◻

</details>

We henceforth use this fixed layer decomposition. A layer of period $p$ receives an additional *budget* $p$. Budgets belong to layers even when several layers happen to share graph vertices.

For a layer $i$, write <a id="eq:first-last"></a>$$\label{eq:first-last}
f_i(x)=z_i(x-1),\qquad l_i(x)=z_i(x)$$ for its first and last ends at start $x$. A higher layer has both functions at least those of a lower layer. When a collection of layers is under discussion, the subscripts $H$ and $L$ denote its highest and lowest layers; a scalar such as $H=f_H(t)$ denotes an end coordinate. [fig:layers](#fig:layers) shows the first and last ends in start–end coordinates. Every actual window on a layer is a graph vertex in $V$; two windows that spell the same word are the same graph vertex, even when they have different coordinates.

<figure id="fig:layers">

<figcaption>A layer in start–end coordinates. An up step appends a letter and increases the end; a down step deletes the first letter and increases the start. At start $x$, the layer visits every window $[x, e)$ between its first end $f_{i}(x)$ and its last end $l_{i}(x)$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-layers"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:layers`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,>=latex,
  layer/.style={line width=.65pt,draw=black!55},
  connection/.style={line width=1.35pt,draw=blue!45!black},
  guide/.style={draw=black!25,densely dotted,line width=.45pt}]

  \begin{scope}[shift={(0,.75)}]
    \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries] at (0,5.3) {A window layer};
    \draw[->,black!65] (0,0) -- (3.9,0) node[below] {start $x$};
    \draw[->,black!65] (0,0) -- (0,4.5) node[above] {end $e$};
    \draw[layer] (.55,1.25) -- (1.6,1.25) -- (1.6,3.05)
      -- (3.1,3.05) -- (3.1,4.05) -- (3.75,4.05);
    \fill[black!65] (1.6,1.25) circle (1.6pt);
    \fill[black!65] (1.6,3.05) circle (1.6pt);
    \node[anchor=north east,inner sep=3pt] at (1.6,1.25) {$f_i(x)$};
    \node[anchor=south east,inner sep=3pt] at (1.6,3.05) {$l_i(x)$};
    \draw[connection,->] (1.25,1.8) -- (1.25,2.55)
      node[midway,left,inner sep=3pt] {up};
    \draw[connection,->] (1.95,3.4) -- (2.8,3.4)
      node[midway,above,inner sep=2pt] {down};
    \fill[blue!45!black] (1.6,2.1) circle (2pt);

    \draw[guide] (1.6,0) -- (1.6,1.17);
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 7 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A 2D diagram in start–end coordinates titled "A window layer". The vertical axis is labeled "end $e$" with an upward arrow; the horizontal axis is labeled "start $x$" with a rightward arrow. Along the horizontal axis, a position $x$ is marked, with a dashed vertical line extending upward. On this line, two end positions are labeled $f_i(x)$ and $l_i(x)$. Between them, a vertical blue line segment with an upward arrow is labeled "up", with a marked point labeled $[x, e)$. At $l_i(x)$, the line steps to the right and downward, labeled "down", continuing as a blue staircase path. Below the diagram, the formulas $f_i(x) = z_i(x - 1), \quad l_i(x) = z_i(x)$ are displayed.
```


<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Unblocked occurrences on layers

Align a recorded occurrence of $s$ on a layer with a template $A$ containing $s$ at position zero. Call it blocked if the interval from the nearest left mismatch to the nearest right mismatch is itself recorded on that layer. Such a block automatically belongs to $V$, since it is a substring of a visited window.

<a id="stmt-lem-matching-window"></a>
<div id="lem:matching-window" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Matching window). *Over all layers, the number of blocked occurrences of $s$ relative to $A$ is $B_A(s;m)$. Every unblocked occurrence is contained in an actual layer window that agrees with $A$ throughout.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matching-window"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matching-window</summary>

*Proof.* Each recorded occurrence of a word $r$ counted in <a href="#eq:block-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-functional">[eq:block-functional]</a>, together with a counted placement of $s$ in it, gives a blocked occurrence. Conversely, the nearest mismatch pair uniquely determines $r$ and its placement. This correspondence commutes with translation by one layer turn, so it is a bijection also for the occurrences counted modulo that turn. [lem:recorded](#lem:recorded) gives the asserted count. Sorting layers did not change that lemma, since it preserved all base edges.

For the second assertion, write the selected occurrence as $[u,v)$. Its first containing window is reached by the up step with end $v$, so that window contains no right mismatch. If it has no left mismatch either, it suffices. Otherwise let $\ell<u$ be the nearest left mismatch. Follow the layer until a down step removes $\ell$; this happens while the windows still contain $[u,v)$, since their starts eventually reach $u$. No right mismatch can enter before that step: together with $\ell$ it would make the nearest-mismatch bracket a recorded interval, contrary to the occurrence being unblocked. The down step introduces no new letter. Immediately afterward no mismatch remains on either side, and the resulting actual window agrees throughout with $A$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:unblocked-count"></a>
In particular, $$\label{eq:unblocked-count}
m(s)-B_A(s;m)$$ counts unblocked recorded occurrences. A layer whose entire text is the aligned template has no blocked occurrences relative to that template.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Two periodicity facts

Periodicity and extremal rotations also enter earlier superstring cycle-cover analyses <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[breslauer1997](#ref-breslauer1997), [mucha2013](#ref-mucha2013)]. We prove the exact agreement and maximal-rotation statements needed here. Use the total order on $\Sigma$ induced by the encoded symbol labels. The lexicographic order of infinite forward words is determined by their first differing letter; equality means agreement at every nonnegative position. In a periodic group, a *distinguished position* $t$ starts its lexicographically greatest forward rotation.

The agreement bound below uses only the weaker $p+q$ consequence of Fine–Wilf periodicity <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[finewilf](#ref-finewilf)]; we retain its direct proof here.

<a id="stmt-lem-agreement"></a>
<div id="lem:agreement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Agreement bounds). *Two distinct aligned periodic texts of periods $p,q$ agree on fewer than $p+q$ consecutive letters. If both have period $p$, they agree on fewer than $p$ consecutive letters.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-agreement"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-agreement</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that a period-$p$ text and a period-$q$ text agree on an interval of length $p+q$. For a full set of $q$ consecutive residues, both positions $i$ and $i+p$ lie in that interval. Equality with the first text therefore makes the second invariant under shift by $p$ at those residues, and $q$-periodicity extends this invariance everywhere. Both texts now have period $p$, and agreement for $p$ consecutive letters makes them equal. The same final observation proves the equal-period bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-max-rotation"></a>
<div id="lem:max-rotation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Maximal rotation). *Let $A$ have least period $p$, and let $t$ be distinguished. Let $D$ be a distinct aligned periodic text of period $q$, all of whose forward rotations are lexicographically at most the forward word of $A$ at $t$. Then $D$ and $A$ cannot agree on all $q$ positions starting at $t$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-max-rotation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-max-rotation</summary>

*Proof.* Set $t=0$ by translating coordinates, and let $S$ be the forward word of $A$. It is at least its shift by $q$. If they are equal, $A$ has period $q$, and any period-$q$ text agreeing on the first $q$ letters is the identical aligned text.

Otherwise let $j\ge0$ be the first index at which $S(j)\ne S(j+q)$. Maximality gives $S(j)>S(j+q)$. Let $U$ repeat the first $q$ letters of $S$. The equalities $S(i)=S(i+q)$ for $i<j$ imply that $U$ and $S$ agree before index $j+q$. At that index, $$U(j+q)=U(j)=S(j)>S(j+q),$$ so $U>S$. If $D$ agreed on the first $q$ letters, its forward word there would be $U$, contrary to the hypothesis on its rotations. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:connections"></a>
### Connection operations

We now construct closed walks that touch existing layers at a cost charged to specified period budgets. We first give joins paid for entirely by the layers being attached, then an operation that also spends the receiving layer’s budget. Each cross-text contact uses an actual word common to the two alignments; the band operation below is restricted to layers of one primitive-text group.

A collection of layers and added walks is *rooted* if its graph support is connected to $\varepsilon$. Connectivity is in the underlying undirected graph; every edge multiset we use is balanced. To *touch* a layer means to share at least one of its graph vertices. Since all base edges remain, touching any vertex of a layer connects to every vertex of that layer.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Following ordered windows

<a id="stmt-lem-ordered-windows"></a>
<div id="lem:ordered-windows" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Ordered windows). *Let $[a,b)$ and $[c,d)$ be windows in a common text over $\Sigma$, each spelling a word in $V$. If $a\le c$ and $b\le d$, there is a hierarchical-graph walk from the first word to the second of cost $d-b$. It visits a word of length at most $\max(0,b-c)$.*

*Consequently an ordered list of windows, with both starts and ends nondecreasing, can be followed at cost equal to the total increase in its ends. If its last window is a translate of the first by $h$ in a text of period dividing $h$, this is a closed walk of cost $h$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ordered-windows"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ordered-windows</summary>

*Proof.* If $c\le b$, delete the first $c-a$ letters, reaching $[c,b)$, and append through $d$. The words on the down path are suffixes of the first word; those on the up path are prefixes of the second. All belong to $V$, and the up cost is $d-b$.

If $c>b$, delete to the empty word. For each letter in the gap $[b,c)$, take an up step to that letter and a down step back to empty. Then build $[c,d)$. Single-letter words in the gap belong to $V$ because the text uses only $\Sigma$. The cost is $(c-b)+(d-c)=d-b$. Concatenating these constructions proves the assertions about lists and translates. ◻

</details>

In particular, a round trip from a vertex $s$ to $\varepsilon$ and back costs $\lvert s\rvert$. Prescribed windows, rather than all intervals they sweep across, must belong to $V$; [lem:ordered-windows](#lem:ordered-windows) supplies the intermediate vertices.

<a id="stmt-lem-band"></a>
<div id="lem:band" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Joining an ordered band). *Consider $k$ ordered layers from one primitive-text group of period $p$. At a start $t$, let $H$ be the top layer’s first end and $E$ the bottom layer’s last end. If $h$ is a nonnegative multiple of $p$ and $H-E\le h$, the layers can be joined together by a closed walk of cost at most $h$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-band"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-band</summary>

*Proof.* If $E\ge H$, every layer contains $[t,E)$: its first end is at most $H$ and its last end is at least $E$. No added walk is needed. Otherwise grow at start $t$ from end $E$ to $H$. Every layer has a vertical range intersecting $[E,H]$, so the growth touches them all. The endpoints are actual bottom and top vertices, and every intermediate word is a prefix of the top vertex. Finish by following to $[t+h,E+h)$, which is a period translate of the starting window. Since $E+h\ge H$, [lem:ordered-windows](#lem:ordered-windows) gives a closed walk of cost $h$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### A collective outgoing link

We call a chosen actual layer window, together with an alignment to a specified periodic text agreeing throughout it, a *record*. A record on a receiving layer provides a vertex at which to attach layers following the other text. An ordinary *link* spends only the attached layers’ budgets. The following link attaches $k$ source layers at combined cost $kp$; it can therefore be planned without reserving any budget of the receiving layer. [fig:collective](#fig:collective) illustrates its construction.

<a id="stmt-lem-collective"></a>
<div id="lem:collective" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Collective link). *Consider $k$ ordered layers with a common period-$p$ text $A$, and put $K=kp$. Let $R=[a,b)$ spell a word in $V$ agreeing with $A$. Let $r_H=f_H(a)$ be the top layer’s first end and $r_L=l_L(a)$ the bottom layer’s last end. If <a id="eq:collective-conditions"></a>$$\label{eq:collective-conditions}
r_H\le b+K,\qquad r_H-r_L\le K,
\qquad r_L+K\ge b,$$ there is a cost-$K$ closed walk based at the word of $R$ that touches all $k$ layers.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-collective"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-collective</summary>

*Proof.* Put $Y=\max(b,r_H)$. Then $b\le Y\le b+K$. For each constituent layer $i$, $$f_i(a)\le Y\le l_i(a)+K=l_i(a+K).$$ Consequently its path meets height $Y$ at a start in $[a,a+K]$. More explicitly, choose the least such start $x_i$ with $z_i(x_i)\ge Y$. If $x_i=a$, then $z_i(a-1)=f_i(a)\le Y$; otherwise minimality gives $z_i(x_i-1)<Y$. Thus $[x_i,Y)$ is an actual vertex. Pointwise layer order makes the $x_i$ nondecreasing when layers are taken from top to bottom. The list from $R$ through these vertices to $R+K$ has nondecreasing starts and ends, and it closes because $K$ is a multiple of $p$. Apply [lem:ordered-windows](#lem:ordered-windows). ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:collective">

<figcaption>A collective link in start–end coordinates. The common height $Y$ meets the ordered layers from top to bottom as the start increases. The endpoints $R$ and $R + K$ spell the same word in the source text, so the path closes at cost $K$. The illustration uses $Y = b$; the precise conditions are in [lem:collective](#lem:collective).</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-collective"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:collective`)
