<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Several requests at one host

A *request* reserves a source layer’s budget for an operation at a receiving layer, called its *host*. Unlike a link, this operation may also spend the host’s budget. It handles all requests at that host together and roots every participating layer. In [sec:processing](#sec:processing), we will send requests only when the following hypotheses hold.

<a id="stmt-lem-host"></a>
<div id="lem:host" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Host requests). *Let a host layer have period $q$. On that layer choose actual windows $R_i=[a_i,b_i)$, indexed by a nonempty collection of distinct child groups. The text of child group $i$ has period $p_i$, is different up to translation from the host’s text and from every other child group’s text, and agrees with the host throughout $R_i$ in the chosen alignment.*

<a id="eq:request-condition"></a>
*For each $i$, one or more child layers request a connection at this same record. Each has a specified actual window $[a_i,e)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:request-condition}
a_i+p_i<e\le b_i-p_i.$$ Its period-$p_i$ budget is reserved for the request. If the host’s period-$q$ budget is also available, the host and all requesting layers can be rooted using at most these budgets.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-host"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-host</summary>

*Proof.* We will spend the host budget on one closed excursion through contact words for the requesting layers. Requesters from all but one child group attach to those contacts using their own budgets. The requesters from the remaining group are touched directly by the host excursion, leaving one of their budgets to connect a contact shorter than their period to $\varepsilon$.

<a id="eq:host-length"></a>
<a id="eq:child-overlap"></a>
By [lem:agreement](#lem:agreement), $$\label{eq:host-length}
b_i-a_i<p_i+q.$$ If record $i$ is before record $l$ along a lift of the host path, their starts and ends are ordered. When $b_i>a_l$, their overlap is an agreement interval between two distinct child texts, so $$\label{eq:child-overlap}
b_i-a_l<p_i+p_l.$$ If there is no overlap, the same strict inequality is immediate.

<a id="eq:host-order"></a>
Choose an index $j$ of minimum $p_j$. Start at its record and order all other records before the next copy of it, translating coordinates by multiples of $q$ as needed. A shift by $\delta\in q\mathbb Z$ also transports that record’s entire child coordinate system: replace its text by $A_i'(x)=A_i(x-\delta)$, its layer functions by $z_i'(x)=z_i(x-\delta)+\delta$, and its requester ends by $e+\delta$. This preserves all graph words, child periods, and budgets; the host text is unchanged because it is $q$-periodic. The same convention applies to the next copy of record $j$ when comparing overlaps. Put $T=a_j+q$. The order gives $$\label{eq:host-order}
a_j\le a_i\le T,\qquad b_j\le b_i\le b_j+q.$$

<a id="eq:host-target"></a>
To construct the host excursion, first visit all specified windows of the requesters at $j$ at start $a_j$, in increasing order of their ends $e$, and then visit $R_j$. For each later record $i$, the raw contact $[a_i+p_i,e)$ is obtained by deleting the first $p_i$ letters of the requester window and lies inside $R_i$. To place these contacts after $R_j$ and before the return at start $T$, we must keep their ends at least $b_j$ and their starts at most $T$. Accordingly, replace the raw contact by $$\label{eq:host-target}
Q_{i,e}=[s_i,h_{i,e})
=[\min(a_i+p_i,T),\max(e,b_j)).$$ Visit these targets in record order and, within each record, in increasing order of $e$. Finally return to the first requester window translated by $q$.

For earlier $i$ and later $l$, <a href="#eq:request-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:request-condition">[eq:request-condition]</a> and <a href="#eq:child-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:child-overlap">[eq:child-overlap]</a> give $$a_i+p_i<e_i\le b_i-p_i<a_l+p_l<e_l.$$ Thus the raw targets have ordered starts and ends. The monotone operations in <a href="#eq:host-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:host-target">[eq:host-target]</a> preserve this order. By <a href="#eq:host-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:host-order">[eq:host-order]</a>, each modified target remains inside its record $R_i$, and it is nonempty because $s_i\le a_i+p_i<e\le h_{i,e}$. It therefore spells a word in $V$ and matches child text $i$.

<a id="eq:host-short-end"></a>
Applying <a href="#eq:child-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:child-overlap">[eq:child-overlap]</a> to $i$ and the next occurrence of $j$ gives $$b_i-T<p_i+p_j.$$ Together with <a href="#eq:host-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:host-length">[eq:host-length]</a>, this implies $$\label{eq:host-short-end}
e\le b_i-p_i<T+p_j,\qquad b_j<T+p_j,
\qquad h_{i,e}<T+p_j.$$ If $e_0$ is the first requester end at $j$, then $e_0>a_j+p_j$, so the final translated window $[T,e_0+q)$ has end greater than $T+p_j$. The whole list is therefore ordered. Its closed excursion costs $q$ by [lem:ordered-windows](#lem:ordered-windows). On its final transition it reaches a vertex of length less than $p_j$. If there are no later records, the same conclusion follows using $b_j<T+p_j$.

<a id="eq:target-child-range"></a>
It remains to attach the other requesters and root the piece. For a requester at $i\ne j$, the target satisfies $$\label{eq:target-child-range}
a_i\le s_i\le a_i+p_i,
\qquad e\le h_{i,e}\le e+p_i.$$ Only the upper end bound needs an additional check. Here the choice of a minimum period $p_j$ is used: if $h_{i,e}=b_j$, then $$b_j<a_i+p_j+p_i\le a_i+2p_i<e+p_i.$$ In child text $i$, follow the ordered list $$Q_{i,e},\quad[a_i+p_i,e+p_i),\quad Q_{i,e}+p_i.$$ The middle word is a period translate of the actual requester vertex; the last word equals the first. The inequalities <a href="#eq:target-child-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-child-range">[eq:target-child-range]</a> make the list ordered. Its cost-$p_i$ loop attaches this requester at the target. Do this separately for all requesters with $i\ne j$.

The requesters at $j$ were already touched in the initial vertical part, so their budgets have not been spent. Use one of their budgets $p_j$ to connect the short contact from <a href="#eq:host-short-end" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:host-short-end">[eq:host-short-end]</a> to $\varepsilon$. The host is touched at $R_j$, all requesters are attached, and the expenditure is at most the host budget plus all requester budgets. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:processing"></a>
### Processing the period groups

We now give the connection procedure. Process the primitive-text groups in increasing order of least period, breaking ties by the lexicographically least rotation of their primitive texts. Requests reserve budgets and are fulfilled when their host group is processed. Ordinary links are planned now and finalized in [sec:cycles](#sec:cycles). Every record and every layer in these plans refers to the fixed base decomposition from [lem:layers](#lem:layers). For bookkeeping, mark a layer rooted when a stated operation explicitly roots it. Incidental connections to $\varepsilon$ do not change these marks or budget assignments.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Incoming requests and a baseline

At entry to a group of period $p$, fulfill all requests received by each recipient layer in one application of [lem:host](#lem:host). We will prove below that every requester has smaller period, and that a child group sends requests using only one record on one recipient layer. These are precisely the facts needed to apply that lemma with fresh recipient budgets.

Every recipient has an actual vertex of length less than $2p$: its record with a child of period $r<p$ has length less than $p+r$ by [lem:agreement](#lem:agreement). If there are recipients, choose their highest layer as a *baseline*. They and their requesters have already been rooted.

Each remaining layer below the baseline can be rooted with its own budget $p$. If it has a vertex of length at most $p$, take a round trip through $\varepsilon$. Otherwise choose a baseline vertex $S=[a,h)$ of length less than $2p$. A lower layer has a vertex $[a,e)$ with $$p<e-a\le h-a<2p.$$ For example, take $e=\min(l_i(a),h)$; pointwise ordering ensures this lies in its vertical range. The windows $$[a,h),\qquad[a+p,e+p),\qquad[a+p,h+p)$$ are ordered because $h<a+2p<e+p$. Their cost-$p$ closed walk touches the lower layer and the rooted baseline. Recipient layers themselves need no further budget.

Let $n$ be the number of layers above the baseline, or the entire number in the group when there is no baseline. Their budgets are all still available. If $n=0$, proceed to the next group.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Easy connections for the upper layers

Use a distinguished position $t$ of the group’s text $A$, and put $$H=f_H(t),$$ the top layer’s first end at that start. If $H-t\le np$, a round trip from $\varepsilon$ through $[t,H)$ costs at most $np$. Its up path touches every upper layer, since every such layer has first end at most $H$. This roots them all.

If a baseline exists and its last end $E$ at $t$ satisfies $E\ge H-np$, [lem:band](#lem:band), including the baseline as the bottom layer and taking $h=np$, joins it to all upper layers within their combined budget. This also roots them all.

<a id="eq:hard-case"></a>
It remains to handle the case $$\label{eq:hard-case}
H>t+np,
\qquad l_{\mathrm{base}}(t)<H-np
\quad\text{when a baseline exists}.$$

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Forcing a record on another aligned text

The count rule will supply $k+1$ unblocked occurrences of a word obtained by shortening the top vertex by $kp$. We choose $k$ so that at most $k$ of those occurrences can have the present full-text alignment. The same choice must keep the top $k$ layers close enough to join for at most $(k-1)p$, leaving one period of their combined budget available. The following threshold search gives both properties.

Starting at $k=1$, increase $k$ while the number of layers of this group satisfying $$l_i(t)\ge H-kp$$ exceeds $k$. Stop at the first failure of this test and set $K=kp$.

<a id="stmt-lem-choose-k"></a>
<div id="lem:choose-k" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sharp-band"></a>
<a id="eq:wide-band"></a>
**Lemma 13**. *This procedure stops with $1\le k\le n$. If $L_*$ is the lowest of the top $k$ layers, then $$\begin{aligned}
l_{L_*}(t)&\ge H-(k-1)p,\label{eq:sharp-band}\\
f_H(x)-l_{L_*}(x)&\le K\qquad(x\in\mathbb Z).\label{eq:wide-band}
\end{aligned}$$ At most $k$ recorded occurrences of $w=A[t:H-K)$ have a full-text alignment identical to $A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-choose-k"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-choose-k</summary>

*Proof.* For $k\le n$, layers at or below the baseline do not qualify, by <a href="#eq:hard-case" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hard-case">[eq:hard-case]</a>. Thus the process stops by $n$. If $k=1$, the top last end is at least its first end $H$. If $k>1$, reaching $k$ means that at least $k$ layers qualified at the previous threshold $H-(k-1)p$. Pointwise ordering gives <a href="#eq:sharp-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-band">[eq:sharp-band]</a>.

For any $x$, choose its translate $x'$ modulo $p$ in $(t-p,t]$. Then $$f_H(x')\le H,
\qquad l_{L_*}(x')\ge l_{L_*}(t)-p.$$ Their difference is invariant under period translation, proving <a href="#eq:wide-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wide-band">[eq:wide-band]</a>.

By <a href="#eq:hard-case" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hard-case">[eq:hard-case]</a>, $H-K>t$, so $w$ is nonempty. Each primitive layer has exactly one position residue whose full-text alignment agrees with $A$: the shifts preserving its text are exactly the multiples of $p$. At that alignment, $w$ is recorded exactly when $l_i(t)\ge H-K$. At most $k$ layers satisfy this inequality when the test stops. Other primitive-text groups have no identical full-text alignment. ◻

</details>

Apply the count rule <a href="#eq:period-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:period-rule">[eq:period-rule]</a>, with origin shifted to $t$, to the actual vertex word $$v=A[t:H),\qquad w=A[t:H-K).$$ The top layer records an unblocked occurrence of $v$ in its own text. By <a href="#eq:unblocked-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unblocked-count">[eq:unblocked-count]</a>, the trigger holds, and the rule gives at least $k+1$ unblocked occurrences of $w$. [lem:choose-k](#lem:choose-k) leaves at least one on a layer $D$ whose aligned text differs from $A$. A different phase of this same group is allowed.

<a id="eq:chosen-record"></a>
<a id="eq:same-group-record"></a>
Choose one such occurrence. [lem:matching-window](#lem:matching-window) gives an actual window $R=[a,b)$ on $D$, agreeing with $A$ throughout and containing the specified interval $[t,H-K)$. Let $q$ be the least period of $D$. Thus $$\label{eq:chosen-record}
a\le t,
\qquad b\ge H-K,
\qquad b-a<p+q.$$ If $D$ belongs to this same primitive-text group, then $$\label{eq:same-group-record}
b-a<p.$$ The strict length bounds follow from [lem:agreement](#lem:agreement). Use this *one* chosen record for every subsequent connection from this processed group.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Distributing the remaining budgets

At start $a$, put $r_H=f_H(a)$ and $r_L=l_{L_*}(a)$. Monotonicity, <a href="#eq:wide-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wide-band">[eq:wide-band]</a>, and <a href="#eq:chosen-record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chosen-record">[eq:chosen-record]</a> give <a id="eq:block-preconditions"></a>$$\label{eq:block-preconditions}
r_H-r_L\le K,
\qquad r_H\le H\le b+K.$$

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### The collective case.

<a id="eq:failed-collective"></a>
If $r_L+K\ge b$, make the top $k$ layers one *collective block*. Plan the link in [lem:collective](#lem:collective) from these layers to $D$ at $R$, reserving their combined budget $K$. Here a collective block is allowed to have $k=1$; its plan is still the collective construction. If the test fails, do not make the block. In that event, $$\label{eq:failed-collective}
r_H\le r_L+K<b.$$

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### The individual cases.

Handle every upper layer not placed in that collective block separately. Its first end at $a$ is at most $b$. When the collective test failed, this follows from <a href="#eq:failed-collective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:failed-collective">[eq:failed-collective]</a>; a layer below the top $k$ has $$f_i(a)\le l_i(t)<H-K\le b$$ by the stopping rule and $a\le t$.

For such a layer, do exactly one of the following.

1.  If some vertex has length at most $p$, root the layer by a round trip through $\varepsilon$ using at most its budget.

<a id="eq:single-short"></a>
2.  If all its vertex lengths exceed $p$ and <a id="eq:single-test"></a>$$\label{eq:single-test}
    l_i(a)+p\ge b,$$ make it an *individual block* with a planned link to $D$ at $R$. At start $a+p$, its vertical range intersects $[b,b+p]$, since $$f_i(a+p)=f_i(a)+p\le b+p,
    \qquad l_i(a+p)=l_i(a)+p\ge b.$$ Choose an actual end $e'\in[b,b+p]$ there and follow <a id="eq:individual-list"></a>$$\label{eq:individual-list}
    [a,b),\qquad[a+p,e'),\qquad[a+p,b+p).$$ This is a cost-$p$ closed walk in $A$ touching the layer. Its incoming transition to start $a+p$ reaches a word of length at most $$\label{eq:single-short}
    \max(0,b-a-p)\le q,$$ by <a href="#eq:chosen-record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chosen-record">[eq:chosen-record]</a> and [lem:ordered-windows](#lem:ordered-windows). Reserve this layer’s budget for that link.

3.  Otherwise reserve the layer’s budget $p$ and send a request to $D$ at the same record $R$. Its first end $e=f_i(a)$ satisfies <a id="eq:outgoing-request"></a>$$\label{eq:outgoing-request}
    a+p<e\le l_i(a)<b-p.$$ This is the condition of [lem:host](#lem:host). In particular $b-a>2p$; combining this with $b-a<p+q$ gives $q>p$. The recipient group will therefore be processed later.

This verifies the promised hypotheses for incoming requests. They always go to strictly larger periods, and a child group uses just one record on one recipient layer, even if several of its layers request there. Ordinary planned links consume no recipient budget. When a recipient is processed, its own budget and all reserved requester budgets are available. After [lem:host](#lem:host) is applied, those layers are rooted and do not enter any outgoing block.

<a id="stmt-prop-partition"></a>
<div id="prop:partition" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14**. *After all groups have been processed, every layer is either marked rooted or belongs to exactly one outgoing block. These two classes are disjoint. Each block consists of layers from one primitive-text group and targets a single actual layer. Its total budget remains reserved for its planned link. A collective block of $k$ layers and period $p$ also satisfies <a href="#eq:sharp-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-band">[eq:sharp-band]</a> at its distinguished position; an individual block consists of one layer.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-partition"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-partition</summary>

*Proof.* Incoming requests use disjoint requester budgets, and each recipient budget is spent only when its group is processed. The baseline step uses only budgets of remaining lower layers. The easy upper-layer branches use at most the combined budgets of the upper layers and finish them. In the remaining branch, the collective case and the individual cases partition those upper layers. Each request is eventually fulfilled, because its recipient is processed later. The layers not marked rooted by a direct operation or a fulfilled request are precisely the outgoing blocks. Their links have only been planned, so their budgets are unspent. The asserted inequality for collective blocks is <a href="#eq:sharp-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-band">[eq:sharp-band]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cycles"></a>
### Opening cycles of planned links

It remains to execute the plans in [prop:partition](#prop:partition). Regard a block as one node and map its target layer to the block containing that layer, or to a rooted sink if the target is marked rooted. Each nonsink node has exactly one outgoing edge. A component therefore reaches a rooted sink or contains one directed cycle. Additional graph intersections between layers can only help; the following argument does not need them to be recorded by this bookkeeping graph.

<a id="stmt-lem-free-period"></a>
<div id="lem:free-period" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (One free period). *A block of $k$ layers and period $p$ can be joined internally, in place of its outgoing link, for cost at most $(k-1)p$. Thus this replacement leaves at least $p$ of its total budget available.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-period"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-period</summary>

*Proof.* For an individual block, or any block with $k=1$, no internal addition is needed. For a collective block, <a href="#eq:sharp-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-band">[eq:sharp-band]</a> gives $$f_H(t)-l_{L_*}(t)\le(k-1)p.$$ Apply [lem:band](#lem:band) with $h=(k-1)p$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-cycle-contact"></a>
<div id="lem:cycle-contact" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (A short contact on a maximal link). *On a directed cycle of blocks, choose a block whose group’s distinguished forward rotation is lexicographically greatest. If its target layer has period $q$, its planned outgoing link can be realized within its reserved budget so that the resulting connected piece contains a vertex of length at most $q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cycle-contact"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cycle-contact</summary>

*Proof.* If the plan is an individual link, <a href="#eq:single-short" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:single-short">[eq:single-short]</a> already gives the contact. For a collective plan with $k$ source layers of period $p$, put $K=kp$. Recall that $t$ is distinguished, $H=f_H(t)$, and the target record $R=[a,b)$ agrees with the source text and satisfies $$a\le t<H-K\le b,\qquad b-a<p+q.$$ If any constituent layer already contains a vertex of length at most $q$, the ordinary collective link connects that vertex to the target and to all other constituents, so it suffices.

<a id="eq:cycle-agreement"></a>
Otherwise every vertex on every constituent layer has length greater than $q$. Every forward rotation of the target’s text is at most its own distinguished rotation, and hence at most the selected block’s distinguished rotation. The target alignment is different from the source alignment by construction. If $b-t\ge q$, agreement on $R$ would include all $q$ positions starting at $t$, contrary to [lem:max-rotation](#lem:max-rotation). Thus $$\label{eq:cycle-agreement}
b-t<q.$$ Put $x=\min(t,a+K)$. We have $a\le x\le a+K$ and $x\le t<b$. If $x=t$, <a href="#eq:cycle-agreement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cycle-agreement">[eq:cycle-agreement]</a> gives $b-x<q$. If $x=a+K<t$, then $$b-x=b-a-K<p+q-K\le q.$$ Thus $[x,b)$ is a suffix of $R$ of length less than $q$.

At start $x$, every constituent layer’s first end exceeds $b$, because its corresponding word has length greater than $q>b-x$. These first ends are all at most $H$: they are no greater than the top first end at $x$, which is at most its first end at $t$. Let $F=f_H(x)$. Then $b<F\le H\le b+K$. Descend from $R$ to $[x,b)$ and grow at start $x$ to $[x,F)$. This growth touches every constituent at its first end, and all intermediate words are prefixes of the actual top-layer vertex. Finish at $R+K$.

In summary, the prescribed list is <a id="eq:cycle-list"></a>$$\label{eq:cycle-list}
[a,b),\qquad[x,b),\qquad[x,F),\qquad[a+K,b+K).$$ Its starts and ends are nondecreasing. Every prescribed word is in $V$, and the last equals the first in the period-$p$ source text. [lem:ordered-windows](#lem:ordered-windows) makes it a cost-$K$ closed walk, with the short contact $[x,b)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-connection"></a>
<div id="thm:connection" class="theorem">

**Theorem 17** (Connecting the base graph). *The base graph can be connected to $\varepsilon$ by adding closed walks of total up cost at most $W$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-connection"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-connection</summary>

*Proof.* Use the group procedure of [sec:processing](#sec:processing). Its fulfilled requests and direct connections have already rooted the layers outside outgoing blocks within their assigned budgets. Consider the remaining functional graph.

For a cycle consisting of one block, its target layer belongs to the same block, hence to the same primitive-text group. The chosen record has a different alignment, so <a href="#eq:same-group-record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:same-group-record">[eq:same-group-record]</a> gives $\lvert R\rvert<p$. Replace the outgoing link by the internal joining of [lem:free-period](#lem:free-period). The target layer, and therefore $R$, lies in this joined piece. The freed budget $p$ pays for a round trip between $R$ and $\varepsilon$.

For a cycle of at least two blocks, choose the outgoing link in [lem:cycle-contact](#lem:cycle-contact); write $q$ for its target block’s period. Retain that selected link, with its contact of length at most $q$. Omit the *target block’s* outgoing link, replacing it by internal joining. This frees $q$ by [lem:free-period](#lem:free-period). The selected link and the omitted link are different, since the cycle has length greater than one. All other cycle links, together with the target’s internal joining, connect the entire cycle into one piece. Use the freed $q$ to root the short contact. [fig:cycles](#fig:cycles) illustrates this choice of the omitted edge.

Distinct directed cycles have disjoint blocks, so these replacements do not compete for budgets. Finally execute the links leading into the rooted pieces, in increasing distance from a root or an opened cycle. Each attaches its source block using only that source’s reserved budget. All layers are now rooted.

Every addition is a closed walk. The initial layer budgets sum to $W$, and each budget was used only in its assigned operation or its cycle replacement. The total added up cost is therefore at most $W$. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:cycles">

<figcaption>Opening a directed cycle of planned block links. Keep a link whose source has maximal distinguished rotation and realize it with a contact of length at most $q$, the target’s period. Omit the target block’s outgoing link and join that block internally, freeing $q$ to connect the contact to the empty word. Arrows between blocks represent planned dependencies, each realized by a closed walk; the dot is a contact vertex, not an extra block.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="sec:algorithm"></a>
### Polynomial time and the output string

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-connection-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-connection</summary>

*Proof of [thm:main](#thm:main).* Parse and preprocess the explicit input in time polynomial in its encoded size $N$. This handles the empty instance and preserves the optimum otherwise. In the nonempty case, construct the counts and base graph of [sec:counts](#sec:counts). By [lem:counts](#lem:counts), [lem:base](#lem:base), its cost is $W\le\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S)$ and it contains every required vertex. Apply [thm:connection](#thm:connection). The resulting graph is balanced, its used support is connected, it includes $\varepsilon$, and its total up cost is at most $2W$.

A finite balanced directed multigraph with connected used support has a closed Euler tour from any used vertex. One may obtain it by following unused edges until a closed walk is formed, and splicing further closed walks at visited vertices until all edges have been used. Balance prevents an unfinished walk from stopping away from its starting point, and connectivity ensures that any unused edges can eventually be reached for a splice.

Start such a tour at $\varepsilon$ and write down the appended letter at each up step. At every step, the current vertex word is a suffix of the string written so far: an up step appends a letter to both, and a down step removes the first letter of the vertex. Every required string is visited and therefore occurs as a substring of the output. Its length is the number of up steps, at most $2W\le2\operatorname{OPT}(\mathcal S)$.

We finish by verifying that all preceding constructions are deterministic polynomial-time operations on explicit finite data. Order encoded symbols, input strings, substring vertices, layers, records, and edge copies by their explicit codes and stable indices. At every finite choice in the construction, including walk decompositions, eligible records, tied extrema, and Euler splices, take the first admissible item in this order. Comparisons and output of variable-width symbol codes are charged their bit cost; no unit-cost operation on an unbounded label or random choice is used.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Counts and graph.

There are $O(L^2)$ words in $V$, each of length at most $L\le N$; store their explicit labels or interned identifiers in a dictionary. For each $v$, enumerate $1\le p<\lvert v\rvert$ and $k\ge1$ with $kp<\lvert v\rvert$. The period-$p$ template is specified by $v[0:p)$, and testing whether it matches $v$ takes polynomial time. Thus all rules <a href="#eq:period-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:period-rule">[eq:period-rule]</a> form a polynomial list. Each blocking functional is evaluated by enumerating $r\in V$ and the possible placements in <a href="#eq:block-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-interior">[eq:block-interior]</a>–<a href="#eq:block-ends" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-ends">[eq:block-ends]</a>. The descending count recursion uses only such evaluations and integer maxima. By [lem:counts](#lem:counts), all counts are at most $L$, so their bit lengths are polynomial as well.

By [lem:base](#lem:base), the explicit base graph has exactly $2W\le2L$ edges with multiplicity; adjacency lists retain individual edge-copy IDs. Its closed-walk decomposition can be found by finite edge traversals. Primitive periods, alignments of texts, and sorted passage lists can be found by direct string comparisons and sorting. The total number of primitive layer phases is the sum of their periods, namely $W$. Store each layer by its primitive period word, one period of exit-end values $z(x)$, and a stable ID; <a href="#eq:layer-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:layer-properties">[eq:layer-properties]</a> recovers other coordinates without enumerating an infinite text.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### Records and connections.

The first and last ends of a layer, its minimum vertex length, and the distinguished rotation of its text can be computed from one period. Equality or lexicographic comparison of two periodic texts requires at most the sum of their periods to find a difference, by [lem:agreement](#lem:agreement). Normalize the start of each considered periodic record to one turn, translating its aligned child coordinates with it as in [lem:host](#lem:host). Each actual window labels a substring of length at most $L$, so the resulting coordinates, lengths, and end differences have polynomial bit length. A record is stored by layer ID, phase, normalized start and end, and its alignment.

The search for a record in [sec:processing](#sec:processing) is also finite. A period-$p$ layer has $p$ up steps and $p$ down steps, hence $2p$ window visits per turn, counting the initial vertex but not its repeated final copy. There are therefore $2W$ such visits over all layers. For the specified $w$ and template $A$, enumerate these actual windows and all placements of $w$ in them. Align each placement with $[t,H-K)$, reject a candidate whose complete aligned text equals $A$, and retain one whose entire window agrees with $A$. There are at most $2WL$ candidates. The count rule and [lem:choose-k](#lem:choose-k), [lem:matching-window](#lem:matching-window) guarantee a covering window on a different aligned text, so this search succeeds.

The host operations, collective links, and internal joins require only ordered lists of windows and the paths of [lem:ordered-windows](#lem:ordered-windows); materialize these as explicit edge lists. Finding the intersections used in [lem:collective](#lem:collective) is a scan along at most $K=kp$ units of start coordinate, with $K$ no larger than the group’s budget and hence at most $W$. Group processing and the final outgoing-block functional graph likewise use polynomially many layers and blocks.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Materializing the answer.

Every added walk is closed, so its number of down steps equals its number of up steps. The total added up cost is at most $W$, and hence the final graph has at most $4W\le4L$ edges with multiplicity. Its explicit construction and Euler traversal are polynomial. This proves both the approximation guarantee and the running-time assertion. ◻

</details>

    {
      "caption": "A layer in start–end coordinates. An up step appends a letter and increases the end; a down step deletes the first letter and increases the start. At start $x$, the layer visits every window $[x, e)$ between its first end $f_{i}(x)$ and its last end $l_{i}(x)$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:layers",
      "pdf_page": 7,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:layers",
          "exact_quote": "A layer in start--end coordinates. An up step appends a letter and increases the end; a down step deletes the first letter and increases the start. At start $x$, the layer visits every window $[x,e)$ between its first end $f_i(x)$ and its l",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\\small,>=latex,\n  layer/.style={line width=.65pt,draw=black!55},\n  connection/.style={line width=1.35pt,draw=blue!45!black},\n  guide/.style={draw=black!25,densely dotted,line width=.45pt}]\n\n  \\begin{scope}[shift={(0,.75)}]\n    \\node[anchor=west,font=\\small\\bfseries] at (0,5.3) {A window layer};\n    \\draw[->,black!65] (0,0) -- (3.9,0) node[below] {start $x$};\n    \\draw[->,black!65] (0,0) -- (0,4.5) node[above] {end $e$};\n    \\draw[layer] (.55,1.25) -- (1.6,1.25) -- (1.6,3.05)\n      -- (3.1,3.05) -- (3.1,4.05) -- (3.75,4.05);\n    \\fill[black!65] (1.6,1.25) circle (1.6pt);\n    \\fill[black!65] (1.6,3.05) circle (1.6pt);\n    \\node[anchor=north east,inner sep=3pt] at (1.6,1.25) {$f_i(x)$};\n    \\node[anchor=south east,inner sep=3pt] at (1.6,3.05) {$l_i(x)$};\n    \\draw[connection,->] (1.25,1.8) -- (1.25,2.55)\n      node[midway,left,inner sep=3pt] {up};\n    \\draw[connection,->] (1.95,3.4) -- (2.8,3.4)\n      node[midway,above,inner sep=2pt] {down};\n    \\fill[blue!45!black] (1.6,2.1) circle (2pt);\n    \\node[anchor=west,inner sep=5pt] at (1.6,2.1) {$[x,e)$};\n    \\draw[guide] (1.6,0) -- (1.6,1.17);\n    \\node[below,inner sep=3pt] at (1.6,0) {$x$};\n    \\node[anchor=north,align=center,font=\\scriptsize] at (1.8,-.55)\n      {$f_i(x)=z_i(x-1)$,\\quad $l_i(x)=z_i(x)$};\n  \\end{scope}\n\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D diagram in start–end coordinates titled \"A window layer\". The vertical axis is labeled \"end $e$\" with an upward arrow; the horizontal axis is labeled \"start $x$\" with a rightward arrow. Along the horizontal axis, a position $x$ is marked, with a dashed vertical line extending upward. On this line, two end positions are labeled $f_i(x)$ and $l_i(x)$. Between them, a vertical blue line segment with an upward arrow is labeled \"up\", with a marked point labeled $[x, e)$. At $l_i(x)$, the line steps to the right and downward, labeled \"down\", continuing as a blue staircase path. Below the diagram, the formulas $f_i(x) = z_i(x - 1), \\quad l_i(x) = z_i(x)$ are displayed."

 }\n\n    \\draw[connection,->] (0,12) -- (9,12);\n    \\draw[connection,->] (9,12) -- (9,21);\n    \\foreach \\j/\\name in {1/top,4/middle,7/bottom} {\n      \\fill[blue!45!black] (\\j,12) circle (2.1pt);\n      \\node[above,inner sep=4pt,font=\\scriptsize] at (\\j,12) {\\name};\n    }\n    \\filldraw[connection,fill=white,line width=.9pt] (0,12) circle (2.4pt);\n    \\node[above left,inner (10.1,21)\n      node[midway,right,inner sep=4pt,align=left,font=\\scriptsize]\n      {$K$ up steps};\n    \\draw[<->,black!65] (0,.55) -- (9,.55)\n      node[midway,above,inner       "witness_description": "A coordinate plot titled \"A collective link through ordered layers\" with a vertical axis labeled \"end\" and a horizontal axis labeled \"start\". An open circle at $(a, Y)$ is labeled $R$, and an open circle at $(a + K, Y)$ is labeled $R + K$. Three staircase-like layer paths, labeled \"top\", \"middle\", and \"bottom\" above their crossings, intersect the line $Y = b$ from left to right. From $R + K$, a vertical line with an upward arrow extends to a solid black circle, annotated with a dimension bracket labeled \"$K$ up steps\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Opening a directed cycle of planned block links. Keep a link whose source has maximal distinguished rotation and realize it with a contact of length at most $q$, the target’s period. Omit the target block’s outgoing link and join that block internally, freeing $q$ to connect the contact to the empty word. Arrows between blocks represent planned dependencies, each realized by a closed walk; the dot is a contact vertex, not an extra block.",

          "exact_quote": "Opening a directed cycle of planned block links. Keep a link whose source has maximal distinguished rotation and realize it with a contact of length at most $q$, the target's period. Omit the target block's outgoing link and join that block",
          "source_object": "fig:0003"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\\small,>=latex,\n  block/.style={draw=black!60,rounded corners=2pt,fill=black!3,\n    minimum width=3.05cm,minimum height=1.15cm,align=center,inner sep=5pt},\n  link/.style={->,draw=black!65,line width=.8pt},\n  selected/.style={->,draw=blue!45!black,line width=1.4pt}]\n  \\node[block] (source) at (0,2.8)\n    {Selected source\\\\$k$ layers, period $p$};\n  \\node[block] (target) at (7.8,2.8)\n    {Target block\\\\$\\ell$ layers, period $q$};\n  \\node[block] (next) at (7.8,0)\n    {Next block};\n  \\node[block] (last) at (0,0)\n    {Another block};\n\n  \\draw[selected] (source.east) -- (target.west)\n    node[midway,below=7pt,align=center,font=\\scriptsize]\n      {retain selected link\\\\cost at most $kp$};\n  \\draw[link] (next.west) -- (last.east);\n  \\draw[link] (last.north) -- (source.south);\n\n  \\draw[->,draw=black!35,densely dashed,line width=.8pt]\n    (target.south) -- (next.north);\n  \\draw[black!70,line width=1.1pt] (7.59,1.21) -- (8.01,1.63);\n  \\draw[black!70,line width=1.1pt] (7.59,1.63) -- (8.01,1.21);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,font=\\scriptsize] at (8.17,1.42)\n    {omit target's\\\\outgoing link};\n\n  \\coordinate (contact) at (3.9,2.8);\n  \\fill[blue!45!black] (contact) circle (2.4pt);\n  \\node[anchor=south east,inner sep=5pt] at (contact) {$s$, $|s|\\le q$};\n  \\node[circle,draw=blue!45!black,fill=white,line width=.9pt,\n    minimum size=.48cm,inner sep=2pt] (root) at (3.9,4.65) {$\\varepsilon$};\n  \\node[above=3pt,font=\\scriptsize] at (root.north) {empty word};\n  \\draw[<->,draw=blue!45!black,line width=1.05pt]\n    (contact) -- (root.south)\n    node[midway,right=5pt,align=left,font=\\scriptsize]\n      {round trip\\\\cost at most $q$};\n\n  \\node[above=6pt,align=center,font=\\scriptsize] at (source.north)\n    {greatest distinguished\\\\rotation on the cycle};\n  \\node[anchor=north,align=center] at (3.9,-.93)\n    {Target budget $\\ell q$: internal joining costs at most $(\\ell-1)q$;\\\\\n     at least $q$ remains to root the short contact.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A directed cycle of blocks comprising four rounded rectangular boxes: \"Selected source\\n$k$ layers, period $p$\" (top left), \"Target block\\n$\\ell$ layers, period $q$\" (top right), \"Next block\" (bottom right), and \"Another block\" (bottom left). Above the \"Selected source\" box is the label \"greatest distinguished rotation on the cycle\". An arrow goes from \"Another block\" up to \"Selected source\". From \"Selected source\", a directed arrow labeled \"retain selected link\\ncost at most $kp$\" leads right to a solid black dot representing a contact vertex labeled \"$s, |s| \\le q$\". From the black dot, an arrow continues right into \"Target block\", while another branch goes upward to a circular node labeled $\\varepsilon$ (\"empty word\"), with an upward arrow and a downward return curved arrow labeled \"round trip\\ncost at most $q$\". The outgoing arrow from \"Target block\" down to \"Next block\" is crossed out with a large $\\times$ mark and labeled \"omit target's outgoing link\". A directed arrow leads from \"Next block\" left to \"Another block\". Below the diagram, an annotation states: \"Target budget $\\ell q$: internal joining costs at most $(\\ell - 1)q$; at least $q$ remains to root the short contact.\""
    }
